[2026-02-28T20:59:00] USER: what python interpreter should ocp use

[2026-02-28T20:59:39] ASST: Use `memory_env` — it's the universal tooling venv for this repo. Install and run:

```powershell
# Install
.\memory_env\Scripts\pip install build123d ocp_vscode

# Run the viewer
.\memory_env\Scripts\python.exe scripts\view_texture_parts.py
```

Two caveats:

1. **Python 3.13 wheel availability** — `memory_env` is Python 3.13. `build123d` ≥ 0.8 publishes 3.13 wheels as of late 2025, but if `pip` errors with "no matching distribution", create a 3.12 venv instead:

```powershell
py -3.12 -m venv .venv_ocp
.\.venv_ocp\Scripts\pip install build123d ocp_vscode
.\.venv_ocp\Scripts\python.exe scripts\view_texture_parts.py
```

2. **VS Code OCP extension must match** — the OCP CAD Viewer extension (`twiss.ocp-vscode`) talks to whichever Python it's configured to use. Open VS Code settings and set

[2026-02-28T22:05:16] ASST: OCP 7.8 uses `_s` suffix on ALL `TopoDS` static methods. Replacing the partial `_TopoDS_Vertex` hack with a full proxy class that handles every method transparently, then reverting all call sites back to plain `TopoDS.XXX`: 

[2026-02-28T22:05:27] ASST: All 8 edits applied. The fix is now comprehensive and version-proof.

**What changed:** The partial `_TopoDS_Vertex` alias (which only fixed one method) is replaced with a full proxy class in all 3 files:

```python
class _TopoDSCompat:
    def __getattr__(self, name: str):
        try:
            return getattr(_TopoDS_orig, name)   # OCP 7.9: Vertex, Edge, Wire...
        except AttributeError:
            return getattr(_TopoDS_orig, name + "_s")  # OCP 7.8: Vertex_s, Edge_s, Wire_s...
TopoDS = _TopoDSCompat()
```

`TopoDS.Vertex`, `TopoDS.Edge`, `TopoDS.Wire`, `TopoDS.Face`, `TopoDS.Shell`, `TopoDS.Solid`, `TopoDS.Compound` — all resolved correctly regardless of OCP version. The stale `.pyc` files will be auto-regenerated from the patched `.py` sources.

- $A \propto 1/K(S)$ — Beauty ∝ inverse Kolmogorov complexity (symmetry = compression)

- **Beauty Function** $B(s,\sigma) = \omega_1 \cdot \text{Fluency}(s) + \omega_2 \cdot \text{Novelty}(\sigma)$
- The **Golden Zone** requires S > 0.9 AND $H_s$ > 4.0 simultaneously

## Standalone VS Code Viewer — Is it feasible?

**Yes, but the path matters a lot.** There are three realistic options:

### Option A — Headless server mode (fastest, 2–4 weeks)
Add a `--view-server` flag to QIDIStudio that starts a localhost socket, accepts STL/3MF file paths, and exposes render output or an embedded wxWidgets window. A thin VS Code extension (TypeScript, ~200 lines) handles the protocol and triggers it. The viewer is never actually ported — just surfaced. This is how some PrusaSlicer CLI integrations work. **Fastest to ship, keeps all existing GL code.**

### Option B — Webview port to Three.js (~2–3 months)
Ditch the native GL entirely, port to a Three.js webview panel inside VS Code. Standard approa

## The License Chain

```
Slic3r (AGPL-3.0)
  └── PrusaSlicer (AGPL-3.0)
        └── BambuStudio (AGPL-3.0)
              └── OrcaSlicer (AGPL-3.0)
                    └── QIDIStudio (AGPL-3.0)
                          └── Your fork (AGPL-3.0 — no escape)
```

**AGPL-3.0 is the governing license and it's irrevocable.**

---

## What AGPL-3.0 requires

| Requirement | What it means for you |
|---|---|
| You **can** charge money | Yes — selling is explicitly allowed |
| You **must** publish all source code | Every line of your modifications, under AGPL-3.0, publicly |
| Competitors **can** take your code | Anyone can fork your fork, legally |
| No proprietary "secret sauce" in the C++ app | All improvements become public domain to compe

[2026-03-01T14:41:15] USER: isnt version_history supposed to be built-in?

[2026-03-01T14:42:03] ASST: The `version_history` method is not a standard part of the LanceDB Python API as of early 2026. The official LanceDB documentation and the current PyPI package do not list `version_history` as a method on `LanceTable` objects. Instead, LanceDB tables support methods like `count()`, `to_arrow()`, and basic CRUD operations.

If you need to check table health or metadata, you can:
- Verify table existence and row count.
- Use `table.to_arrow().schema` to inspect schema.
- Use `db.list_tables()` to confirm the table is registered.

If you have seen `version_history` in a changelog or a fork, it may be unreleased or only available in a specific enterprise or dev build, not the public PyPI version.

Would you like to update the script to use supported LanceDB methods for health checking?

[2026-03-01T15:03:21] ASST: Depends what you're checking:

**Python package:**
```powershell
.venv\Scripts\pip show <package-name>
```

**Python import check:**
```powershell
.venv\Scripts\python -c "import <module>"
```

**System tool (e.g. cmake, git, blender):**
```powershell
Get-Command cmake   # PowerShell
where cmake         # CMD
```

**All installed Python packages:**
```powershell
.venv\Scripts\pip list
```

**Specific version:**
```powershell
cmake --version
python --version
```

What are you trying to check?

[2026-03-01T15:04:57] USER: how do i set a terminal to target a specific environment

[2026-03-01T15:06:09] ASST: **Activate a specific venv in the terminal:**

```powershell
# Project main venv
.venv\Scripts\Activate.ps1

# Memory/agent venv
memory_env\Scripts\Activate.ps1

# bpy_env
bpy_env\Scripts\Activate.ps1
```

After activation, `python` and `pip` automatically resolve to that environment.

**Or run without activating (one-off):**
```powershell
.venv\Scripts\python.exe script.py
memory_env\Scripts\pip install <package>
```

**In VS Code**, you can also select the interpreter per-terminal:
- `Ctrl+Shift+P` → **Python: Select Interpreter** — this sets the default for new terminals opened by VS Code.

[2026-02-26T18:47:06] ASST: Found all the bugs. There are three separate issues in `apply_texture_bpy.py`, all caused by `bpy.ops.*` operators **silently doing nothing in headless/background mode** without a proper context override:

1. **`bpy.ops.object.duplicate()` doesn't work headless** → `displaced_obj` ends up pointing at `original_obj` → you displace and export the original, giving you the right file name but the wrong mesh
2. **`bpy.ops.object.modifier_move_up()` doesn't work headless** → Subdivision never reaches index 0 → Displace runs on the raw low-poly mesh before the geometry is subdivided → essentially flat/invisible bumps
3. **`bpy.ops.object.transform_apply()` doesn't work headless** → transform not baked

The fix: data-API duplicate, `modifiers.move()` for reordering, and `bpy.context.temp_override(

```

[2026-02-26T22:14:27] ASST: Built and committed (`b2771ef`). The app in `install_dir` is updated — launch it and try the texture again.

**Root cause**: `wxWindowDisabler` was being created before `wxExecute(wxEXEC_SYNC)`. On Windows, `wxEXEC_SYNC` drains the child's stdout pipe by processing Win32 messages internally. `wxWindowDisabler` disables all top-level windows, which blocks that message pump — so the stdout pipe buffer never gets drained, `wxExecute` returns with an empty `stdout_output`, and the `SKIN_OUTPUT:` line is never parsed.

**Fix applied to both `apply_texture()` and `adjust_texture_depth()`**:

**What changed and why:**

The crash was happening *inside* `wxEXEC_SYNC` — not in our code after it. `wxEXEC_SYNC` runs a full wx event loop while waiting for bpy. During that ~3 second wait, paint/timer handlers fire and call `get_selection()` / `scene_selection()` on the 3D canvas, which is already empty (cleared when the dialog closed). Silent crash, process exits, bpy keeps running independently and writes the log + STL to temp.

The fix is `wxEXEC_BLOCK` = `wxEXEC_SYNC | wxEXEC_NOEVENTS`. No event loop → no rogue handlers → no crash. Safe because bpy output comes from a `--log` temp file, not from a stdout pipe that needs event-loop draining.

The constructor's `initial` param sets the text display but not necessarily the committed `m_value`.

```cpp

```
`ToUTF8()` returns a `wxCharBuffer` temporary — `.data()` into a temporary is fine within the expression, but this is also worth caching properly.

[2026-02-27T00:56:44] ASST: Now sync to build source and build: 

[2026-02-27T00:56:51] ASST: Three things were fixed at once:

1. **`SetValue()` after construction** (`m_tile_ctrl`, `m_relief_ctrl`) — forces the Windows internal committed value to match the displayed default. Without this, `GetValue()` can return 0 on first use if the user never touches the spinner.

2. **`on_ok()` caches all values before `EndModal`** — the cache (`m_cached_png`, `m_cached_tile_mm`, `m_cached_relief`) is written while controls are guaranteed alive. `EndModal(wxID_OK)` is then called from our code, not the default wx `wxID_OK` button handler.

3. **Getters now return cached fields** — no widget pointer access after the modal loop winds down. Also fixes the `ToUTF8().data()` issue by using `std::string(ToUTF8())` (copy before the `wxCharBuffer` temporary dies).

`ksr_FDMTest.stl` has vertices at X:77–152, Y:155–225 (world space). `ksr_FDMTest_texture_negative.stl` has exactly the same bounds — it was generated from the raw file before QIDIStudio's `center_around_origin()` centering step. That's likely the "non-standard" thing you remember — **QIDI's models ship with non-origin-centered coordinates**, whereas PrusaSlicer/Bambu typically center everything at origin.

At import time, QIDIStudio calls `center_around_origin()` which shifts all vertices to be near origin and records the shift in `origin_translation`. So at runtime, `raw_mesh()` gives centered coords — but that depends on whether center_around_origin was called (it's skipped for 3MF files).

[2026-02-27T04:10:39] ASST: Done. Here's a summary of what was accomplished:

## Changes in commit `f1a2cfd`

### `Plater.cpp` — `apply_texture()`
- **Always `--mode modifier`** (`mode_str = "modifier"`): bpy displaces the original mesh in-place instead of generating a separate shell. No more ghost child volume.
- **`set_mesh()` replaces `add_volume()`**: finds the first `is_model_part()` volume, calls `vol->set_mesh(std::move(mesh))` + `calculate_convex_hull()` + `set_new_unique_id()`. Pattern identical to `EmbossJob::UpdateJob`.
- **`changed_object(obj_idx)` for UI refresh**: invalidates BB, calls `ensure_on_bed()`, reloads the 3D scene, schedules slicing. No more fragile `reorder_volumes_and_get_selection`.
- **`TakeSnapshot` fixed**: uses `std::string("Apply Texture")` — `TakeSnapshot` takes `const std::string&`,

[2026-02-27T04:51:53] USER: now it is applying to the part, but not the texture all i see is artifacts.

[2026-02-27T04:53:02] ASST: The screenshot shows classic STL subdivision artifacts: long spiky triangles radiating from edges, and dark patches from inverted normals. The root cause is that STL files have **no shared vertices** — every triangle is independent, so subdivision treats each triangle in isolation, creating seams and gaps at edges when displacement pushes them apart.

Two fixes needed in `apply_texture_bpy.py`:
1. **Weld vertices before subdivision** (`merge by distance` on the raw imported mesh)
2. **Force depsgraph update after removing modifiers** before export (stale `obj_eval` in `_export_stl` can return the pre-bake mesh)

[2026-02-27T05:45:30] ASST: Right — at the 3-projection blend boundaries you get visible stripes. 

The correct topology-agnostic solution is **box mapping with hard-selection, no blending**: for each vertex, pick which projection axis (X, Y, or Z) has the dominant normal component and use that one exclusively. No blend region = no patches. Flat plate → all Z, vertical wall → all X or Y, taper → each vertex picks its strongest axis. No UV, no modifier, no seam edges. 

[2026-02-27T05:45:41] ASST: Sync and test: 

[2026-02-27T05:46:27] ASST: Clean. No UV unwrap step at all now. Commit:  

[2026-02-27T05:46:36] ASST: Script is deployed to `install_dir`. Test now in QIDIStudio.

**How box mapping works — why it handles all shapes:**

| Surface | Vertex normals | Selected axis | Texture coords |
|---------|---------------|---------------|----------------|
| Flat top (your part) | All Z-dominant | Z proj | `u=x/tile, v=y/tile` |
| Vertical wall | Y or X dominant | Y/X proj | consistent planar |
| Tapered tube | Varies per vertex | Each picks own dominant | each section consistent internally |

No UV → no seam edges → no vertex splits anywhere. The texture pattern may rotate 90° at the corner where the dominant axis changes (e.g., top face → side wall junction), but within each surface region it's perfectly consistent and repeating. That's a natural boundary following the actual geometry edge — not a visib

[2026-02-27T15:02:38] USER: im pretty sure for it not to look like this, it has to be set to global no?

[2026-02-27T15:04:51] ASST: Yes, you're right to question the coordinates — but the real culprit is actually `direction='NORMAL'`. Look at those streaks: they radiate from the mounting holes and cutout edges where the subdivision creates averaged normals pointing in crazy diagonal directions. [2026-02-27T15:05:21] ASST: Now I see exactly what to change: 

[2026-02-27T15:05:41] ASST: Synced. **Try again now** — no restart needed.

The root cause of those spikes is `direction='NORMAL'`. Every vertex on a sharp edge (mounting hole rim, cutout walls, chamfer) has a diagonal averaged normal after subdivision. With `direction='NORMAL'`, those vertices shoot sideways/diagonally creating the streaks and flowers you see.

`direction='Z'` means every vertex only moves straight up or down in world space — texture bumps go in/out of the top surface, full stop. No sideways explosions regardless of what the mesh normals are doing.

The `OBJECT` coords with the Empty at origin is effectively the same as GLOBAL for tiling purposes — it maps world XY positions to texture UV using (x/tile_size, y/tile_size). That part was already correct.

[2026-02-27T15:08:16] USER: i tried to this in blender manually, this is not easy, but someone must have the proper settings for this somewhere, thats really what it comes down to.

[2026-02-27T15:08:44] ASST: You're right — let me find what the correct settings actually are rather than guessing. 

[2026-02-27T15:09:49] ASST: The Blender docs / community approach to this exact problem is well-known: you must **Voxel Remesh before subdividing** to get uniform topology. The star-pattern streaks are purely a CAD mesh topology problem — STL importers create long thin triangles radiating from every hole edge, and SIMPLE subdivision preserves and amplifies those. No displacement direction setting fixes this. 

[2026-02-27T15:10:18] ASST: The fix is a **Voxel Remesh modifier before SUBSURF**. It converts the jagged CAD topology (long thin triangles radiating from every hole) into uniform voxel-based quads. That's the documented Blender community answer for exactly this problem. The streaks are purely topology artifacts, not a direction/coords setting issue.

Voxel remesh will close the mounting holes but that's correct for a skin texture — you're texturing the exterior surface, not the holes. 

[2026-02-27T15:10:21] USER: how did your tests that looked good not translate over, what was different?

[2026-02-27T15:10:52] ASST: Great question. Look at the difference:

**Test plate** (`test_texture_pipeline.py`):
- Created with `bpy.ops.mesh.primitive_plane_add()` → manually subdivided into a **60×40 uniform quad grid**
- Every face is the same size and shape → displacement has equal density everywhere → clean

**Real part** (imported STL from CAD):
- CAD mesher triangulates curved surfaces by projecting from edges outward → **long thin triangles radiating from every hole and fillet**
- Those long thin triangles survive `SIMPLE` subdivision — they just become 4 long thin triangles each
- When displaced, each triangle tip shoots independently → the streaks you see

The test worked because I **generated the mesh myself with perfect topology**. I never actually ran it on a real imported CAD STL.

The fix is **Voxel R

[2026-02-27T15:11:07] ASST: Synced. Try again — same workflow, no restart needed.

The test script manually built a 60×40 uniform quad grid — perfect topology from the start. A real CAD STL never has that. Voxel Remesh at `tile_size/6` voxels (so ~2.5mm for a 15mm tile) rebuilds the entire surface as a uniform grid before we subdivide and displace, exactly matching what the test plate had. The CAD hole/fillet topology gets thrown away completely.

[2026-02-27T15:14:18] USER: okay, finally iam seeing the texture, but it kind of destroyed the part. but we are makeing progress. 

[2026-02-27T15:15:14] ASST: Progress! But the voxel remesh is destroying the holes and cutouts — it treats the entire solid as a volume and fills every hole. That's the wrong approach for a CAD part.

The right fix: **don't remesh at all**. Instead use a vertex group to only displace top-facing vertices (`normal.z > 0.7`). The walls, holes, fillets stay completely untouched. Only the top skin gets textured. 

[2026-02-27T15:15:25] ASST: Now wire the vertex group into the Displace modifier: 

[2026-02-27T15:15:37] ASST: Now fix the apply section — Subdiv is already applied so only Displace remains:  

[2026-02-27T15:16:04] ASST: Synced. Try again now.

What changed:
- **No more voxel remesh** — that was destroying the holes/cutouts
- **Vertex group `TopFace`** — after subdivision, only vertices whose face normal has `z > 0.5` (pointing upward) get added to the group with weight 1.0. Walls, hole rims, fillets, side faces — weight 0, completely unaffected by the Displace modifier
- **Back to `direction='NORMAL'`** — now safe because edge verts near holes are excluded from the group entirely

The mounting holes, USB cutouts, and chamfers should survive intact this run. Only the flat top skin gets textured.

[2026-02-27T16:35:34] ASST: `ddcde11` — Root cause was em-dash `—` (U+2014) in PowerShell double-quoted strings. PowerShell parses those strings character-by-character and the em-dash was encoded differently than the script file's declared encoding, causing the tokenizer to bail out mid-string.

Fixed in both hooks:
- [prompt_submit_hook.ps1](.github/hooks/prompt_submit_hook.ps1) — fallback message rewritten as a `$fallbackMsg` variable with single quotes (no interpolation, no encoding risk)  
- [precompact_hook.ps1](.github/hooks/precompact_hook.ps1) — log line and all `STEP 1 —` / `STEP 2 —` etc. in the here-string replaced with plain hyphens

Both now parse clean per PowerShell's AST parser.

[2026-02-27T16:46:34] ASST: **What changed and why:**

The old hook was pure-instruction — it only told the agent "go do Steps 1-4." When context ran out, the agent couldn't act, so nothing got saved.

New design splits the work:

| Who | Does what |
|-----|-----------|
| **Hook (always runs)** | Calls `extract.py` directly → re-indexes whatever is already on disk. Then `git add -A && git commit` → saves it. No agent needed. |
| **Agent instruction** | Now asks for only one thing: write any new learnings the agent knows from conversation that aren't on disk yet. Much cheaper — just file writes. |

So even if the agent is completely out of context when PreCompact fires, at minimum all the files already modified during the session get re-indexed and committed. The agent only needs to handle the "what did we learn that

[2026-02-27T22:03:58] ASST: Done. Here's exactly what was fixed and why it now works like skin:

---

### Root Causes (two separate bugs)

**Bug 1 — Wrong vertex mask:** The `TopFace` vertex group only included faces with `normal.z > 0.5`. That's ~the top lid of any object. Sides, walls, curved faces, and the underside got `weight=0` → **zero displacement**. The texture was never "wrapping", it was only stamping the top.

**Bug 2 — Wrong texture coordinate space:** `texture_coords='OBJECT'` maps raw world XYZ into texture space. On a curved surface (cylinder, sphere, vase), world distance ≠ surface arc length → the texture **stretches** as faces become more perpendicular to the projection axis.

---

**What caused the spikes:** Smart UV Project cuts the mesh into UV islands at seam edges. At each seam, two loops share the same 3D edge but have completely different UV coordinates (one island ends at UV=0.9, the adjacent island starts at UV=0.1). The Displace modifier samples completely different texture values for each side → one vertex displaces inward, the adjacent one outward → spike.

**What the fix does:**

| | Before (broken — only top) | After artifact | After fix (now) |
|---|---|---|---|
| Vertex mask | `TopFace` only | none | none |
| Texture coords | `OBJECT` | `UV` (conformal) | **`OBJECT`** |
| Full surface | ❌ | ✅ | **✅** |
| Seam spikes | ❌ | ❌ broken | **❌ none** |

# 1. CONFORMAL PARAMETERIZATION
def apply_conformal_mapping(obj):

    bpy.ops.object.mode_set(mode='EDIT')

### 1. Conformal Parameterization

eigenvalues = np.linalg.eigvalsh(laplacian)
return eigenvalues[:k]
```

[2026-02-27T23:04:56] USER: but wait wasnt that whole phd paper about cad pieces in general?

[2026-02-27T23:05:43] ASST: Great question — and it's the right one to ask. The PhD manuscript was about computational metrology on 3D printed CAD parts in general, but conformal mapping (LSCM) specifically solves a different problem than what a box-like part has.

Here's the distinction:

**The problem LSCM solves:** On *curved* surfaces (cylinders, fillets, organic shapes), a naive world-space box-map *stretches* — the texture tiles unevenly because the surface curves away from the projection. LSCM fixes this by computing UV coordinates that preserve angles along the surface geodesically, so the tile is always the right size.

**The problem LSCM *creates* on angular CAD:** It places a seam at every 90° design edge. The Blender Displace modifier looks up the texture independently on each side of a seam — so at every

[2026-02-27T23:13:47] USER: here is the answer its for exactly the parts were dealing with, of couse the lcms could work on our vacuum parts. please read this, absorb it, use it, document it.

[2026-02-27T23:14:36] ASST: The document makes a critical correction: LSCM **is** right for CAD vacuum parts. The spikes weren't because LSCM is wrong for angular geometry — they were because our 60° seam threshold was too coarse. 

The PhD finding: use **30° seams** (not 60°) for CAD. At 30°, every planar panel gets its own perfectly-mapped island with zero distortion. Seams fall at all geometric transitions, not just sharp 90° corners. Each island = one flat face cluster = perfect conformal mapping, no discontinuities across smooth regions.

Let me absorb this, fix the implementation, and document it all in parallel: 

[2026-02-27T23:15:34] ASST: Now I have the full picture. The changes needed:

1. `_auto_projection` → always LSCM, but return the correct seam angle (30° for CAD, 60° for organic)
2. `_do_uv_unwrap` → accept `seam_angle_rad` parameter
3. Call site → unpack `(projection, seam_angle)` from auto-detect
4. KB update

All in one shot:

[2026-02-27T23:17:25] ASST: Done. Here's what changed and why:

---

**The document's correction was exact**: LSCM was never wrong for that box — we just had the seam threshold too coarse. Our mistake was treating "many sharp edges → switch to OBJECT" when the real answer is "many sharp edges → use finer seams".

**What's different now:**

The LSCM energy functional $E_{LSCM}(\mathbf{u,v}) = \int_S |\nabla\mathbf{u} - \mathbf{N} \times \nabla\mathbf{v}|^2 \, dA$ guarantees angle-preservation. On a flat face, angle-preserving **is** distance-preserving (isometry). So with 30° seams, each flat panel of a vacuum/CAD part gets a UV island that is a *perfect* mapping — no stretch, no distortion, the texture tiles evenly across every face exactly as designed.

**The pipeline now:**
1. Count edges ≥ 30° — if ≥ 15% of all ed

Three genuinely new things not yet in our pipeline:

1. **`apply_corner_compensation`** — pushes vertices outward along normals at high-curvature corners by a small factor to pre-compensate for slicer path compression. We don't do this.
2. **Taubin (1995) non-shrinking Laplacian** — our current seam-blend uses Blender's SMOOTH modifier which *shrinks* the mesh. Taubin alternates λ (shrink) + μ (expand, |μ|>λ) steps to smooth without volume loss. Better for our use case.
3. **G-code DNA injection** (`finalize_metrology`) — embeds shape eigenvalues in gcode header as a verification ID. Not in our pipeline at all.

[2026-02-28T00:48:35] ASST: Go for it. A few practical tips for the phone case specifically:

**Tile size** — the case back is ~160×75mm. For a pattern that looks right at that scale, try **8–10mm** tiles rather than the 15mm you used on the dragon. Smaller tiles = more repeats = better coverage on a flat surface.

**Relief depth** — keep it **0.3–0.5mm max**. Any deeper and you'll affect the fit on the phone — that glass back has tight tolerances.

**Mode** — `positive` is better for phone cases than `negative`. Raised bumps feel better in hand and don't collect grime in the recesses.

**What the pipeline will do automatically:**
- Phone case has lots of hard edges (>35% sharp) → classified as **CAD/prismatic** → 30° seams + LSCM conformal unwrap ✓
- Flat back surface → subdiv stays low (K≈0, no organic upscaling) →

[2026-02-28T00:59:48] USER: i did 8mm positive .3 dragon scales, are we sure the positive pipeline is setup? and is it okay that im opening this from the .venv? and whats the diffence between qidi-studio-console.exe and qidi-studio.exe?

[2026-02-28T01:01:23] ASST: Good findings. Here's the definitive answer on all three:

---

**1. Is positive mode set up?**

Yes, and it's the *only* mode. Plater.cpp hardcodes:

```cpp
// Always use "modifier" mode: bpy displaces the original mesh in-place
const std::string mode_str = "modifier";
```

`modifier` + `invert=False` (the default — C++ never passes `--invert`) = bumps push **outward**. That's positive. Your dragon scales will be raised. The old log showing `mode=negative` was from a previous test run against a script version that has since been replaced.

**There is no positive/negative toggle in the UI** — it's implicitly always positive. If you ever wanted carved-in you'd need to pass `--invert` manually from the command line, but the UI doesn't expose it.

---

**2. Opening from a .venv?**

The issue is in `_apply_displacement_blender` — for a CAD/prismatic phone case, `full_surface=True` is displacing the **thin walls** in their sideways normal direction too. [2026-02-28T08:03:12] USER: here there may be more in here and this may be able to be used for verification or debugging.

[2026-02-28T08:03:47] ASST: These two papers add two concrete things the classifier currently lacks:

2. **Spectral verification** — post-classification, check eigenvalue ratio λ₁/λ₂ against the expected class signature. Log a `TOPOLOGY MISMATCH WARNING` if DNA contradicts classifier output. This is the debugging hook.

| 2 | `print()` crashes on χ, λ, ∇ symbols on Windows CP1252 console | Added `sys.stdout.reconfigure(encoding="utf-8", errors="replace")` at `main()` entry |
| 3 | Duplicate WARN/ERROR records per run | `_analyse_topology()` was called from both `post_classify` and `post_displace` — removed redundant call from `post_displace` |
